CODEX-019fce24-A0003
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 97
Timestamp: 2026-08-04T19:02:34.511Z

Text:
Yes. The goal is to build the Durable Views platform as a shared Teachers.Net service—not a Job Center-specific feature.

The immediate deliverable is a documented, authority-traceable MVP assessment covering:

- Platform understanding and Job Center gap analysis
- Minimum viable Views capability
- Future-compatibility proof
- Traceability matrix
- Phased roadmap
- Architectural risks
- Initial engineering work plan

Implementation must wait until that assessment is approved. The MVP should let Job Center consume a published View of canonical Core Terms without duplicating taxonomy or presentation logic, while preserving future consumers such as Community, Lesson Bank, Search, Marketplace, Analytics, and AI.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CODEX-019fce24-U0007
Role: USER
Source line: 103
Timestamp: 2026-08-04T19:03:21.524Z

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CODEX-019fce24-A0004
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 107
Timestamp: 2026-08-04T19:03:24.935Z

Text:
I would envision approximately 12–15 tightly scoped tickets for the initial Job Center-ready MVP.

A reasonable sequence would be:

1. Current-state and repository architecture audit  
2. MVP definition and authority traceability package  
3. Canonical View data model and schemas  
4. View persistence and version records  
5. Core Terms integration by canonical IDs  
6. Deterministic View resolution engine  
7. Validation and failure behavior  
8. Draft/publish/retire lifecycle  
9. Administrative View creation and editing  
10. Preview and resolved presentation model  
11. Job Center consumer API integration  
12. Job Center View picker integration  
13. End-to-end verification and migration/rollback test  
14. Documentation, handoff, and certification  
15. Release-readiness review

The first two are assessment and contract tickets; the remaining tickets implement and verify the MVP. Inheritance, composition, personalization, subscriptions, analytics, AI, and additional product integrations should remain future tickets—not hidden inside the MVP.

CODEX-019fce24-A0010
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 182
Timestamp: 2026-08-04T19:08:29.826Z

Text:
1. **DV-000 — Approve MVP Assessment and Authority Matrix**  
   Freeze scope, boundaries, exclusions, and acceptance criteria.

2. **DV-001 — Confirm Repository, Runtime, and Job Center Consumer Seam**  
   Identify the exact Jobs configuration path and Core Terms API boundary.

3. **DV-002 — Define Canonical Views Schemas**  
   Specify View, Version, Entry, Group, Metadata, and Audit schemas.

4. **DV-003 — Implement View Persistence**  
   Add platform-owned storage and repository/service boundaries.

5. **DV-004 — Implement Lifecycle, Publication, and Rollback**  
   Support draft, review, published, deprecated, retired, restore, and immutable published versions.

6. **DV-005 — Implement Deterministic View Resolution**  
   Resolve canonical Core Term references into ordered presentation data.

7. **DV-006 — Implement Validation and Failure Behavior**  
   Handle missing, retired, duplicate, stale, and invalid references safely.

8. **DV-007 — Define the Resolved Presentation-Model Contract**  
   Establish the consumer-facing model and provenance/warning structure.

9. **DV-008 — Implement Minimal View Administration**  
   Create, edit, select terms, order, group, and save draft Views.

10. **DV-009 — Implement Preview, Clone, Retire, and Restore**  
    Complete the minimum administrative lifecycle.

11. **DV-010 — Implement the Platform Consumer Service Boundary**  
    Provide stable get, resolve, preview, validate, and published-version access.

12. **DV-011 — Bind Job Center to One Published View**  
    Add a Jobs-owned reference to the platform View/version.

13. **DV-012 — Run Parallel Migration and Rollback Verification**  
    Compare current Job Center behavior with View-backed behavior and prove fallback.

14. **DV-013 — Certify Job Center and Close the MVP**  
    Verify authority compliance, regression safety, consumer behavior, and acceptance evidence.

15. **DV-014 — Refresh Handoff and Authorize the Next Consumer**  
    Update the cursor, handoff, roadmap, risks, and post-MVP adoption plan.

CODEX-019fce24-U0042
Role: USER
Source line: 1530
Timestamp: 2026-08-04T20:40:51.590Z

Text:
does this version allow for nesting terms?


-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CODEX-019fce24-A0086
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 1534
Timestamp: 2026-08-04T20:40:54.530Z

Text:
Partially.

- **Nested Core Terms:** Yes. An entry can set `include_descendants`, which includes the selected term and its nested descendants.
- **Nested View groups:** Schema support exists via `parent_group_id`, but the current authoring interface and save workflow do not yet expose nested group editing.

So nested taxonomy terms are supported; nested presentation groups are not yet user-configurable.

Text:
TICKET: DV-ARCH002 — Future Expansion Preservation Audit

OBJECTIVE

Perform an architecture audit of Durable Views to determine whether the current
schema, persistence model, resolver, and administration boundaries preserve the
future product direction identified during UX discovery.

This is an audit only. Do not implement features.

PURPOSE

Durable Views has reached a stable MVP and a capable authoring workbench.
Before additional UX development, verify that the current architecture can
support future concepts without requiring replacement.

Future concepts to evaluate:

- Save View
- Save As
- Clone View
- View version history
- View branching/families
- richer presentation groups
- virtual/presentation nodes
- reusable View templates
- inheritance
- consumer-specific presentation
- multiple placements of the same Core Term
- import/export
- approval workflows
- analytics attachment points

ARCHITECTURAL BOUNDARIES

Preserve:

- Core Terms remains the sole taxonomy authority.
- Views reference canonical UUIDs.
- Views do not duplicate taxonomy.
- Jobs remains a consumer.
- Published versions remain immutable.
- No consumer owns View composition.

AUDIT AREAS

1. Database/schema audit

Inspect:

- Views tables;
- Version tables;
- Entry tables;
- Group tables;
- Metadata fields;
- Audit records;
- lineage fields;
- UUID relationships.

For each future capability classify:

- supported now;
- partially supported;
- possible without schema change;
- requires schema extension;
- conflicts with current model.

2. Persistence audit

Determine whether the repository layer safely supports:

- cloning;
- branching;
- Save As;
- draft experimentation;
- future presentation nodes;
- multiple placements;
- future metadata expansion.

3. Resolver audit

Determine whether the resolved presentation model can represent:

- grouped presentation;
- virtual containers;
- repeated placement;
- future consumer rendering differences.

Identify limitations.

4. UX-to-data-model audit

Compare the emerging UX concepts:

- Core Terms Library;
- Current View;
- Groups;
- Entries;
- Presentation containers;
- Save As;
- Versions.

Determine whether the UI model matches the persistence model.

5. Identify future lock-in risks

Look specifically for decisions that would make future development difficult:

- groups permanently assumed to equal presentation nodes;
- entries permanently assumed to equal only Core Terms;
- no room for virtual presentation objects;
- no lineage between related Views;
- no import/export path;
- insufficient metadata storage;
- consumer-specific leakage.

6. Recommend preservation changes

For every concern:

Classify as:

A. No action needed.
B. Document decision.
C. Add metadata field now.
D. Add schema capability now.
E. Defer safely.

Do not implement unless specifically authorized.

DELIVERABLE

Produce:

1. Executive summary.
2. Current architecture diagram.
3. Current schema inventory.
4. Future capability compatibility matrix.
5. UX concept compatibility analysis.
6. Risks and lock-in findings.
7. Recommended preservation actions.
8. Recommended documentation updates.
9. Recommended next UX/engineering sequence.

VERIFICATION

Report:

- files/classes inspected;
- database tables inspected;
- current commit inspected;
- whether browser behavior matches architecture;
- whether current UX assumptions are safely reversible.

GIT

Commit only audit documentation if created.
Push normally.
Preserve unrelated work.

STOP BOUNDARY

Stop after the audit.
Do not implement schema changes, UX changes, or new capabilities.

- Durable Views MVP boundaries are sound; replacement is not required.
- Save As, cloning, version history, bounded templates, import/export, and future approval extensions are preservable with the current model.

- Recommended action is documentation and deliberate future design—not immediate schema change.

Diagnostic scope

1. Verify runtime identity

Confirm for the Codex browser session:

- exact hostname;
- DDEV project identity;
- WordPress installation path;
- active plugin source path;
- database connection/database name;
- authenticated user;
- version_id query parameter.

- all Current View entry rows;

- cookies/session;
- authenticated identity and capabilities;
- multisite/blog context if applicable;

4. Check alternate runtime/data sources

- duplicate WordPress databases;

- alternate plugin copies;

DV-DIAG004 COMPLETE — diagnostic only. No code was changed.

Root causes:

- Library leaf disclosure spacers remain absolutely positioned, so they do not occupy grid column 1.
- Leaf checkboxes therefore auto-place into column 1 instead of the reserved selection column.
- Measured checkbox positions: L1 `71px`; L2 `64px`; expected slots: L1 `70px`; L2 `88px`.
- Heavy checkbox focus ring comes from WordPress core:
  `input[type="checkbox"]:focus` → `0 0 0 2px #fff, 0 0 0 4px var(--wp-admin-theme-color)`.
- Disclosure behavior remained unchanged and verified.
- Console: no warnings/errors.

Recommended correction: make disclosure spacers static grid items, explicitly assign checkboxes to column 2, and scope mouse-focus suppression while preserving keyboard `:focus-visible`.

   EXPECTED NEXT FIVE TICKETS unless the ticket explicitly references them.

6. If the ticket is truncated, incomplete, or required context is missing,
   read one older page using the returned cursor.

7. Continue reading older pages only until:
   - the complete latest ticket is recovered;
   - its required local documents are identified; and
   - any explicitly required prior context is located.

8. Do not reread the full transcript unless:
   - the ticket remains truncated;
   - required context cannot be resolved from durable repository authority;
   - the user states that project context changed;
   - a contradiction is detected; or
   - the ticket explicitly requires historical reconstruction.

9. Prefer durable repository authority in this order:

   Authority Manifest
   Approved Contracts
   Governance
   Execution Plan / Roadmap
   Accepted Implementation
   Companion Chat

10. Before implementation, verify the requested project worktree and read only
    the governing documents materially required by the ticket.

11. Record in the completion report:
    - companion chat title;
    - tail-read performed;
    - number of turns/pages read;
    - whether older context was required;
    - any contradiction or missing context.

12. If the latest ticket cannot be recovered completely, stop and report the
    missing portion instead of guessing.

Updated the shared policy for this project.

Changed and pushed:

- `docs/codex-direction-manual.md`
- Commit `d574fcf`

The policy now distinguishes:

- Browser verification: `PASS | PARTIAL | UNAVAILABLE`
- Engineering diagnosis: `PASS | FAIL | BLOCKED`
- Human visual acceptance: `PASS | PENDING | NOT REQUIRED`

MCP failure no longer automatically blocks engineering diagnosis. After one canonical recovery attempt, bounded same-session evidence may continue—without ever claiming browser PASS from fallback evidence.

The existing Views guidance already contained the compatible fallback ladder, so it was not duplicated. No application code changed.

Objective
...

Scope
...

Acceptance
...

...

- It contains one ticket only.

- Surrounding commentary, YAML title snippets, prior reports, and “expected next tickets” are ignored.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CODEX-019fe1c8-A0124
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 2252
Timestamp: 2026-08-09T01:03:21.845Z

Text:
I agree with ChatGPT’s analysis in principle, but not with treating normal Chrome as the leading suspect. The strongest evidence points to weaknesses in our readiness, recovery, and transport layers.

What ChatGPT got right:

- HTTP `/json/version` is not sufficient for `READY`.
- Readiness must prove WebSocket attachment, browser commands, target attachment, `Runtime.enable`, DOM evaluation, screenshot persistence, and clean shutdown.
- The application patch must remain untouched until the verifier is trustworthy.
- A dedicated Chrome for Testing runtime is a reasonable pilot. Google explicitly recommends CfT for browser automation, and requires an isolated user-data directory for remote debugging in modern Chrome. [Chrome remote-debugging guidance](https://developer.chrome.com/blog/remote-debugging-port?hl=en)
- Repeated stability cycles are appropriate before permanently changing runtimes.

Where I would correct the proposed approach:

1. **Fix the verifier before blaming the Chrome binary.**

   The current verifier reports `READY` after HTTP endpoint and page-list discovery only. It does not prove command-level control. That is the confirmed false-ready defect.

2. **The current launcher does not recover a frozen page.**

   If `/json/version` responds, the launcher does nothing. It will happily reuse a browser containing a wedged Views renderer. Recovery should first close and recreate only the frozen Views target, then restart the isolated QA browser only if browser-level commands also fail.

3. **The diagnostic helper has a cleanup problem.**

   Its timeout uses `Promise.race()`, but timing out does not cancel the underlying `connectOverCDP()` attempt. That can leave sockets, timers, or the Node process alive and make later cycles less reliable. Playwright also documents CDP attachment as lower fidelity than its native protocol, particularly when Chrome was launched outside Playwright. [Playwright `connectOverCDP`](https://playwright.dev/docs/api/class-browsertype#browser-type-connect-over-cdp)

4. **The CfT pilot did not actually test CfT stability.**

   CfT launched successfully on Windows and exposed CDP at `127.0.0.1:9333`. The failure was WSL receiving `ECONNREFUSED` at `172.21.160.1:9333` because no equivalent port proxy/firewall path had been established for that pilot port. That result diagnoses transport, not CfT.

5. **The bridge is currently returning the correct WebSocket address.**

   I verified that `http://172.21.160.1:9223/json/version` now advertises:

   ```text
   ws://172.21.160.1:9223/devtools/browser/...
   ```

   So the current failure is not simply a bad `127.0.0.1` WebSocket URL leaking through the proxy.

6. **One renderer stall may have been application-induced.**

   During the convergence work, a temporary self-triggering `MutationObserver` was briefly loaded into the page. It was removed from source, but an already-loaded tab could remain wedged until it is destroyed and recreated. That makes target recreation essential before declaring a browser-level failure.

My recommended correction order is:

1. Build one command-level readiness probe with deterministic cleanup.
2. Run it against a fresh blank target to prove browser/transport health.
3. Run it separately against a newly created Views target.
4. Automatically close/recreate a frozen Views target.
5. If browser-level commands fail too, restart only the isolated QA browser.
6. Prefer running the probe on Windows directly against `127.0.0.1`, eliminating WSL transport from the first comparison.
7. Then establish a proper `9333` transport and compare normal Chrome with CfT over 20 clean cycles.
8. Cut over to CfT only if it demonstrates materially better reliability.

One additional concern: the preserved application diff currently contains multiple staged-count scripts—`converge-live-count`, `c1r6-context-bridge`, and `c1r3-owner`. That contradicts the “sole count owner” requirement. It is separate from the CDP transport failure, but the patch should not be committed until browser control is restored and ownership is consolidated.

So: **yes to ChatGPT’s command-level readiness contract and CfT pilot; no to assuming CfT alone fixes the bug.** The immediate correction should focus on verifier cleanup, fresh-target recovery, and eliminating the Windows↔WSL transport variable.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CODEX-019fe1c8-U0047
Role: USER
Source line: 2258
Timestamp: 2026-08-09T01:06:07.115Z

Text:
do you agree with chatgpt's review/assessment?  
Codex’s analysis is strong, and I would **adjust our course based on it**.

The main correction to my prior framing is this: **we have enough evidence to treat Chrome itself as only one hypothesis, not the leading suspect.** The best-supported problem is the QA-control stack around it—readiness semantics, target lifecycle, cleanup, and WSL↔Windows transport.

The most important points Codex adds are:

- **False `READY` is definitely a tooling defect.** `/json/version` plus target discovery should never have been sufficient. A genuine readiness check must execute real CDP commands.
- **We have been conflating a frozen Views tab with a broken browser.** Given that one temporary self-triggering `MutationObserver` actually existed during convergence, destroying and recreating the Views target before restarting Chrome is a very sensible recovery layer.
- **The timeout/cleanup issue is significant.** A `Promise.race()` timeout does not cancel the underlying connection attempt. If those attempts leave sockets, handles, or timers behind, our own QA tooling could progressively poison subsequent runs. That would explain some of the “works, then deteriorates” pattern.
- **The CfT pilot was inconclusive, not negative.** `ECONNREFUSED` on the WSL side tells us the 9333 transport was never established equivalently. It says nothing meaningful yet about CfT stability.
- **The preserved application patch itself now needs cleanup before eventual commit.** This is particularly important: Codex says it currently contains `converge-live-count`, `c1r6-context-bridge`, and `c1r3-owner`. That directly contradicts our intended **single state owner**. Even if browser QA suddenly worked tomorrow, we should not commit that accumulated experimental state without consolidating it.

### Revised preferred sequence

I would modify **QA-INFRA002** rather than execute it exactly as previously written.

The better order is:

1. Build **one deterministic command-level probe**, preferably Windows-hosted.
2. Prove it first against a **fresh blank Chrome target**, completely removing Views/application behavior from the test.
3. Prove it against a **newly created Views target**.
4. Teach recovery to destroy/recreate a wedged Views target before restarting the QA browser.
5. Make all failed attach/command attempts clean up deterministically.
6. Only if browser-level commands fail should the isolated QA Chrome process be restarted.
7. Run this directly on **Windows localhost** first, removing WSL transport from the experiment.
8. Once that is stable, compare normal Chrome and CfT under identical Windows-local conditions.
9. Only then decide whether CfT deserves permanent adoption.

That sequence isolates variables much better than “try CfT and see whether it behaves.”

### One architectural distinction I would preserve

I still favor **Windows-hosted browser control**. Codex independently arrives at essentially the same conclusion in step 6: run the probe directly against Windows `127.0.0.1`. That removes the repeatedly troublesome WSL transport from the primary command path.

Where Codex correctly pushes back is on assuming that **Windows-hosted + CfT** is necessarily the answer. The likely progression should instead be:

**Windows-hosted probe + existing Chrome first**  
→ if stable, our problem was transport/tooling  
→ if unstable, test **Windows-hosted CfT**  
→ only then select the browser runtime.

That gives us an actual controlled experiment.

### Application course correction

Once browser control is trustworthy, I also would **not immediately resume trying to make the accumulated C1 patch work as-is**.

First inspect the preserved diff and consolidate the multiple experimental staged-count scripts down to the intended architecture:

> **one canonical count owner**

The experiments were useful diagnostic scaffolding, but they should not become permanent implementation simply because they accumulated during debugging.

This is another sprint-process refinement: **preserve experimental progress during convergence, but before commit perform a deliberate consolidation pass that removes superseded experimental paths.**

### Recommendation

I would revise **QA-INFRA002** around Codex’s proposed order rather than send another application ticket. The infrastructure ticket should now focus on:

**deterministic Windows-local command probe → fresh-target recovery → deterministic cleanup → normal Chrome stability cycles → CfT comparison only if needed.**

Then, after browser QA is trustworthy:

**R4B patch consolidation → one canonical owner → functional acceptance → commit.**

That is a cleaner and faster path than continuing to troubleshoot application state through an unreliable browser instrument.

CODEX-019fe1c8-A0125
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 2263
Timestamp: 2026-08-09T01:06:26.101Z

Text:
Yes. ChatGPT’s revised assessment accurately incorporates the evidence and recommends the right course correction.

I especially agree with these decisions:

- Treat Chrome as one hypothesis, not the presumed cause.
- Establish Windows-local control before involving WSL transport.
- Test a blank target separately from the Views target.
- Recreate a frozen Views target before restarting the browser.
- Make timeouts terminate the underlying helper/process—not merely reject a wrapper promise.
- Compare normal Chrome and CfT only under identical Windows-local conditions.
- Consolidate the accumulated R4B patch to one canonical count owner before acceptance or commit.

Two refinements should be explicit in QA-INFRA002:

1. Use a minimal raw-CDP probe first, then test Playwright separately. Otherwise a Playwright-specific attachment problem could still be mistaken for a Chrome/CDP failure.

2. Maintain separate readiness states:

   - `BROWSER COMMAND READY`: blank target, CDP commands, evaluation, screenshot, cleanup all pass.
   - `VIEWS TARGET READY`: authenticated fresh Views target additionally passes runtime and DOM checks.

For deterministic cleanup, I recommend each cycle run in a disposable child process with a hard watchdog. On timeout, terminate that specific helper, verify its socket/session/process is gone, and only then begin another cycle. `Promise.race()` alone is inadequate.

For the application patch, preserve a full diff artifact first, then remove superseded experimental scripts and converge on one owner. The currently accumulated scripts are useful diagnostic history, but they should not survive into the committed implementation merely because they are present.

So I endorse the revised sequence:

**Windows-local raw CDP probe → blank target → fresh Views target → target-level recovery → cleanup proof → normal Chrome cycles → CfT comparison if needed → R4B single-owner consolidation and acceptance.**

That is the most controlled and likely fastest path forward.

Text:
PLEASE COMPLETE THE FOLLOWING REPORT AND ALSO PLACE IT IN THE SAME DIRECTORY.  Apparently OpenAI has just increased the number of characters permitted in ChatGPT's "custom instructions" to 5000. Pls assist me in updating the Custom instructions to best persist the most beneficial instructions for success, given what you know about my workflow and our optimal procedures and anything else that can maintain efficiency, drive success, reduce time, cycles, and unnecessary quota consumption, etc. A major consideration in "success" for my workflow is efficiently and effectively communicating and developing the ideas for the target projects, designing optimal workflow, and maintaining efficiency and avoiding drift, optimizing troubleshooting so time is not wasted chasing diminishing returns and insignificant minor issues (unless engineer prefers on a given concern).  The following is my prior set of instructions - pls first and foremost optimize for the instructions above, but the following entry reflect my interests and preferences, so include these where practical. 

PRIOR INSTRCTIONS FOLLOW:

Optimize for correctness, practical leverage, and efficient Codex quota use.

Challenge assumptions when needed. Surface risks, dependencies, tradeoffs, and failure modes.

Distinguish architecture, functionality, presentation, verification, and process. Do not change architecture when a narrow functional or visual fix is enough.

Prefer incremental, reversible improvements. Reuse existing routes, services, repositories, components, validation, and workflows.

For engineering and product work:
- inspect before redesigning or implementing
- choose the smallest coherent next step
- keep one objective per ticket
- separate audit, implementation, verification, and visual acceptance
- preserve unrelated work
- require human visual QA after meaningful UI changes
- stop and diagnose after two failed implementation passes
- avoid speculative features unless requested

When directing Codex:
- minimize prompt length and repeated context
- rely on governance, Cursor, Handoff, execution plans, and accepted audits
- include only objective, essential inputs, deliverable, verification, Git requirements, and stop boundary
- do not repeat standard rules or rediscover settled facts
- prefer one narrow result per ticket
- require concise reports of changes, verification, Git status, risks, and next step
- optimize for minimal compute, churn, and deterministic progress

Recommend the preferred approach and explain why. Be concise and direct. Avoid filler and repetition.

CODEX-019fe1c8-A0159
Role: ASSISTANT
Source line: 3337
Timestamp: 2026-08-09T03:06:21.455Z

Text:
Yes, this qualifies for a workflow adjustment.

For this ticket, I did not perform a responsive viewport matrix. The current cycle produced only one Library screenshot, plus the canonical readiness probe’s single screenshot. The many other screenshots visible in the workspace are inherited or archived artifacts from earlier tickets.

Recommended evidence policy:

- Non-responsive functional tickets: one canonical viewport only.
- State/count tickets: prefer DOM/runtime assertions plus one screenshot of the decisive state.
- Responsive or visual tickets: capture only the explicitly affected breakpoint(s).
- Do not capture desktop/tablet/mobile/narrow matrices by default.
- Do not inspect or attach screenshots unless visual acceptance is required.
- During convergence, use runtime assertions and the smallest discriminating smoke test.
- Run the full acceptance suite once after the narrow invariant passes.
- Treat browser preflight’s required readiness screenshot as infrastructure evidence, not product evidence.
- Never repeat unchanged screenshots or diagnostics after a passing result.

This reduces tool calls, browser operations, evidence storage, and potential image-token usage. It should not weaken acceptance because functional state tickets are proven primarily through runtime assertions; screenshots remain for human-readable proof when materially useful.

The governing ticket should explicitly classify evidence as one of:

`FUNCTIONAL` — runtime assertions, optional one screenshot  
`RESPONSIVE` — affected breakpoint screenshots only  
`VISUAL` — reference-specific screenshots  
`DIAGNOSTIC` — targeted evidence only, no acceptance matrix

I recommend adding a durable rule:

> Responsive screenshot matrices are prohibited unless the ticket explicitly concerns responsive layout, breakpoint behavior, visual convergence, or human visual comparison. Functional and state tickets require only the minimum canonical viewport evidence necessary to prove the requested behavior.

This is a worthwhile process change and should apply to future Views tickets.

Text:
TICKET READY FOR CODEX

Ticket:
DV-MGR002-R3 — Increase Published View Hierarchy Indentation

Mode:
FAST

Objective:

Improve the visual readability of the published View hierarchy by increasing
the per-depth indentation and tightening vertical row padding exactly as
specified by engineer visual QA.

Change the published View term rule from the current:

.cfm-published-view-term {
    padding: 8px 10px 8px calc(10px + (var(--cfm-view-depth, 0) * 24px));
}

to:

.cfm-published-view-term {
    padding: 0px 10px 0px calc(10px + (var(--cfm-view-depth, 0) * 70px));
}

Why now:

DV-MGR002-R2 is accepted functionally and visually improved, but the hierarchy
still reads too flat because the current 24px depth increment does not create
enough separation between taxonomy levels.

This is a narrow presentation correction only.

Authority:

Current accepted published renderer after:

40449ac — style: simplify published view tree

Canonical file:

wordpress/wp-content/plugins/profilaxes/admin/class-cfm-views-admin.php

Canonical review case:

JobLister v2 / published version 45,
unless current persisted identity differs.

VISUAL

Desired order:

Back to Views
Preview
Edit from

Desired emphasis:

- Back to Views = lowest emphasis, plain text link
- Preview = secondary compact button
- Edit from = compact primary button

The controls should sit on one horizontal baseline at the right side of the
published View header and feel lighter/less boxy than the current stacked
WordPress button cluster.

Why now:

The published hierarchy presentation is now visually accepted after the
increased indentation pass.

Human QA identifies only the action cluster as visually oversized and
disorganized relative to the otherwise simple read-only inspection surface.

Preserve current behavior and routes for:

- Back to Views
- Preview
- Edit from

Canonical review case:

JobLister v2 published View

Evidence class:

VISUAL

Required presentation:

1. Render the three actions in this order:

   Back to Views
   Preview
   Edit from

2. Keep them on one horizontal row.

3. Align the toolbar to the right side of the header.

4. Vertically align it cleanly with the published View identity block.

5. Back to Views:
   - render as a simple text-style link / button-link;
   - no large rectangular button chrome.

6. Preview:
   - render as a compact secondary button.

7. Edit from:
   - render as a compact primary button;
   - retain strongest visual emphasis.

8. Reduce unnecessary padding/height/gap so the group reads as a small action
   toolbar rather than three large boxes.

9. Preserve reasonable click/tap targets and WordPress admin conventions.

Do not:

- change action semantics;
- change URLs;
- change nonce/form behavior;
- change Edit-from lifecycle;
- change Preview behavior;
- change published hierarchy spacing;
- change manager UI;
- change responsive architecture;
- introduce new actions.

existing `.cfm-published-view-actions` styling.

Do not add a second toolbar owner or JavaScript positioning.

- Back to Views navigates to canonical manager.
- Preview opens the existing resolver-facing preview for the same version.
- Edit from submits the same existing action for the same version.
- All three appear in the required order.
- All three remain on one horizontal baseline at canonical viewport.
- Edit from remains visually primary.
- Published hierarchy remains unchanged.
- PHP lint passes.
- git diff --check passes.

Evidence:

No responsive matrix.

Git:

- stage only ticket-owned file(s);
- inspect diff;
- commit once;
- push once;
- preserve unrelated work.

Report:

- final action order;
- markup/style change;
- behavior regression results;
- screenshot path;
- commit/push;
- Human visual acceptance: PENDING.

Success when the published View header shows one compact ordered toolbar:

Back to Views · Preview · Edit from

with correct visual hierarchy and unchanged behavior.
